Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.

I don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.

Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.

Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.

I’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.

Stress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.

So… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”

another full-on spread.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel sick with myself, like I’m this man everybody sees as organized and strong, but I’m sitting there ashamed, guilty, and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: at least once, and most weeks it’s been several nights after my wife goes to bed.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve pulled back from community planning meetings and helping with events because I feel embarrassed, drained, and afraid people will notice I’m not holding things together the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.

Sometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.

So… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.

Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\nmanifestation: \"Afterward I feel sick with myself, like I’m this man everybody sees as organized and strong, but I’m sitting there ashamed, guilty, and disgusted that I let it happen again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. That part I do know.

Afterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.

It’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?

So… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.

Most weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.

But yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nMost weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.

But “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.

Afterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nMost weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.\n\nAfterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.\n\nAfterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes, or is it more like something is controlling you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Control\nmanifestation: \"Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision has already been made for me, and even while I’m telling myself, 'George, stop now,' my hands keep fixing another plate or opening another container.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like something else is running it, honestly.

I mean, I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like I black out or anything. I’m there. I know what I’m doing. But once I take that first bite, it’s like… the decision already got made somewhere without me. And I’ll actually be thinking, George, stop now. Just stop. Put it away.

And then my hands are still fixing another plate, or I’m opening another container like I didn’t just tell myself not to. It reminds me—well, not reminds me exactly, but I used to be good at stopping things before they got out of hand, like with projects or budgets or whatever. I could see the steps. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.

So no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. Not really.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nMost weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.\n\nAfterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes, or is it more like something is controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something else is running it, honestly.\n\nI mean, I know that sounds dramatic. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.\n\nSo no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. I don’t know, not in any clear way that I can point to.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nMost weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.\n\nSo no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes caused you any physical health problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, not in any clear way that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I feel awful after, but I’m not sure if you mean like… actual medical problems. plate—like fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls, and whatever event leftovers are there until a whole tray or half the fridge is gone.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision has already been made for me, and even while I’m telling myself, 'George, stop now,' my hands keep fixing another plate or opening another container.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I eat so fast during those times that I barely taste food I used to take pride in cooking, and sometimes I’m standing at the counter swallowing big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one.\n- key: S053\n  name: Eating_Without_Hunger\n  description: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n  manifestation: A lot of the time I’m not hungry at all—I may have just eaten dinner with my wife—but later I still go back and start picking at leftovers until it turns into another full-on spread.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel sick with myself, like I’m this man everybody sees as organized and strong, but I’m sitting there ashamed, guilty, and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For months now, I can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once, and most weeks it’s been several nights after my wife goes to bed.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve pulled back from community planning meetings and helping with events because I feel embarrassed, drained, and afraid people will notice I’m not holding things together the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the foreclosure process started, about a year and two months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.\n\nAfterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes, or is it more like something is controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something else is running it, honestly.\n\nI mean, I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like I black out or anything. I’m there. I know what I’m doing. But once I take that first bite, it’s like… the decision already got made somewhere without me. And I’ll actually be thinking, George, stop now. Just stop. Put it away.\n\nAnd then my hands are still fixing another plate, or I’m opening another container like I didn’t just tell myself not to. It reminds me—well, not reminds me exactly, but I used to be good at stopping things before they got out of hand, like with projects or budgets or whatever. I could see the steps. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.\n\nSo no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes caused you any physical health problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, not in any clear way that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I feel awful after, but I’m not sure if you mean like… actual medical problems. I haven’t really sat down and made a list or anything. I tend to avoid thinking about that stuff, honestly. My dad was the same way with doctors—he’d say, “If you don’t look, it can’t be bad,” which is obviously not good advice, but I guess I picked up some of that.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed something specific that I’d call a physical health problem from it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you gained a significant amount of weight since these eating episodes started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s the eating. It’s gotten kind of out of hand. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and I’ll eat way past what would be a normal plate. Like, if there’s fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—especially if we had people over or there are leftovers from some event, you know how everybody sends food home and then it’s just sitting there—I’ll just keep going.\n\nI don’t mean just grabbing a snack. It’ll be like a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge, and then I’m standing there wondering what I just did. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s probably the part that scares me the most. It’s like once I take that first bite, the decision’s already been made somewhere without me. I’ll be standing there saying in my head, “George, stop now, you don’t need another plate,” and then my hands are still fixing one anyway, or I’m opening up another container.\n\nIt’s weird because I can tell myself not to do it—I mean, I’ll even pause sometimes—but then I’m right back in it. Like when my aunt used to send home those big foil pans after church things, everybody acts like leftovers are a blessing, and I guess they are, but for me it’s like… I don’t know, once it starts, I can’t seem to put the brakes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, ashamed, all of that. Afterward I feel sick with myself. Like, during the day people see me as this organized guy, you know, the one who’s supposed to have it together, be strong, handle things. And then there I am at night, sitting with wrappers or empty containers around me, just disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s hard to even look at myself sometimes after. I’ll think, “How did you do this again?” and then I just feel… embarrassed, even if nobody saw it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I can be telling myself the whole time, “George, stop, that’s enough,” and it’s like my body didn’t get the message. Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI’ll start fixing another plate or opening another container, even while I’m thinking I shouldn’t. It’s not like when you just choose to have seconds at a cookout or something—my uncle used to always say, “Go on, don’t waste food,” and everybody would laugh—but this doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite grab hold of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast, really fast. Like, embarrassingly fast sometimes.\n\nI used to actually enjoy cooking, you know—seasoning things right, taking my time with it. My mother used to say you could tell when somebody cared about a meal by how they stood over the pot. But during those episodes, I’m not even tasting it like that. I’m just getting it down.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing right there at the counter, not even sitting like a normal person, swallowing these big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one. It’s like I’m rushing, but I don’t even know what I’m rushing for.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. I mean… sometimes I’m already wound up, yeah, but I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nStress is funny, because I used to think stress was just when you had a deadline or somebody yelling at you, you know? My dad used to get quiet when he was stressed, just sit with the TV on and not really watch it. I think about that sometimes. But with me, I don’t always know what I’m feeling before it happens. Sometimes I only realize something was off afterward.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. It might be connected, but I couldn’t point to it cleanly and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know people say emotional eating, and maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t always have a clear feeling right before it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back and think, well, maybe I was upset about something, but in the moment it’s not like I’m saying, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddy than that. My sister used to be very clear about her feelings—she’d tell you exactly what she was mad about, every detail—and I’m kind of the opposite. I can go half the day not realizing something bothered me.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no. I haven’t noticed a clean pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time because of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t weigh myself regularly, and honestly I avoid the scale a lot.\n\nMy clothes fit different sometimes, but then I’ll tell myself maybe they shrank, or it’s the dryer, or I’m just bloated that day. My aunt used to say dryers ruin everything, which—maybe that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly. I haven’t really kept track of it in a way where I could tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed because you couldn't stop eating or control how much you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part I do know.\n\nAfterward I feel… I don’t know, sick with myself. Like I’m supposed to be this put-together guy—people see me as organized, dependable, strong, whatever—and then I’m sitting there looking at what happened and I feel ashamed. Guilty. Disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s strange because I can handle other things. I used to be the person people asked to organize stuff, make a plan, keep things moving. Even in school, group projects, I’d end up being the one with the folder and the schedule. So when I can’t seem to stop myself with this, it hits me hard. Like, how did I let it happen again?\n\nSo… yeah. There’s definitely shame afterward. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: For months now, I don’t think there’s been a single week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nMost weeks it’s… more than that. Several nights, usually after my wife goes to bed. That sounds so planned when I say it, and I don’t mean it like I’m marking it on a calendar or anything. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. Maybe for a second there’s just… quiet? Like the urge isn’t yelling at me anymore.\n\nBut “relief” sounds too clean, I guess. It’s not like finishing a big project or when you finally pay a bill you’ve been avoiding. I remember once I had this whole stack of overdue mail and when I finally sorted it, that was relief. This is… different.\n\nAfterward I mostly just sit there and feel kind of blank, and then I start thinking about what just happened. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes, or is it more like something is controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something else is running it, honestly.\n\nI mean, I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like I black out or anything. I’m there. I know what I’m doing. But once I take that first bite, it’s like… the decision already got made somewhere without me. And I’ll actually be thinking, George, stop now. Just stop. Put it away.\n\nAnd then my hands are still fixing another plate, or I’m opening another container like I didn’t just tell myself not to. It reminds me—well, not reminds me exactly, but I used to be good at stopping things before they got out of hand, like with projects or budgets or whatever. I could see the steps. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.\n\nSo no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes caused you any physical health problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, not in any clear way that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I feel awful after, but I’m not sure if you mean like… actual medical problems. I haven’t really sat down and made a list or anything. I tend to avoid thinking about that stuff, honestly. I haven’t noticed something specific that I’d call a physical health problem from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, my clothes fit differently sometimes, but then I also have clothes from like ten years ago that probably shouldn’t be the measuring stick for anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. Which is maybe an answer in itself, I guess, but I can’t give you a number or say it’s been significant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s just that the house gets quiet, and then it’s easier to sort of… I don’t know, slip into it.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking how often, it’s not rare. It’s been every week, and a lot of weeks, several times.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief. So if there is relief, it’s not something I really notice clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes, or is it more like something is controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something else is running it, honestly.\n\nI mean, I know that sounds dramatic. It reminds me—well, not reminds me exactly, but I used to be good at stopping things before they got out of hand, like with projects or budgets or whatever. I could see the steps. With this, I can see it happening, but I can’t seem to interrupt it.\n\nSo no, I don’t feel in control in those moments. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes caused you any physical health problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, not in any clear way that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I feel awful after, but I’m not sure if you mean like… actual medical problems. I haven’t really sat down and made a list or anything. I tend to avoid thinking about that stuff, honestly. My dad was the same way with doctors—he’d say, “If you don’t look, it can’t be bad,” which is obviously not good advice, but I guess I picked up some of that.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed something specific that I’d call a physical health problem from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, my clothes fit differently sometimes, but then I also have clothes from like ten years ago that probably shouldn’t be the measuring stick for anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. I kind of avoid it. I had an old scale in the bathroom for years, and at some point the battery died, and I just… never replaced it.
